#### 🆕 `p576` — the region now has instruments, and its own-language open-source supply is a hobby tier

🟢 **See this pass's section at the top of this page for the six jurisdictions and
`Ley 2626 de 2026`.** What belongs here is the consequence: **the mandates are arriving in
jurisdictions whose local-language open-source supply cannot meet them.**

🔴 **Measured first-hand this pass, with denominators read off GitHub's own result counts:**

| axis | repositories | top of the star ladder |
| --- | --: | --- |
| `topic:educacion` (Spanish) | **410** | 🔴 **`71★`** — a JavaScript course, not a product |
| `topic:educacao` (Portuguese) | **378** | 🟢 **`722★`** — `portabilis/i-educar`, a real platform of record |

🔵 **The two languages are not in the same condition, and that is the finding.**
🔴 **Spanish-language supply has no platform tier at all**: below the `71★` course
repository sit teaching materials, a Moodle attendance plugin for **Chile's SENCE** platform
(`29★`), and a Spanish vocational-training grading app (`9★`). 🟢 **Portuguese-language
supply does have one** — `portabilis/i-educar` (**`722★`, `GPL-2.0`**, default branch
**`2.12`**), with `i-diario` (**`117★`, `AGPL-3.0`**, branch **`1.6`**) and `ipti/br.tag`
(**`20★`, `GPL-2.0`**) around it — 🔴 **and every one of them is copyleft.**

🟢 **Brazil also has the one artefact in this whole base that a curricular mandate can be
validated against**, and it is permissive where it counts:
**[`bncc-dev/bncc-dados`](https://github.com/bncc-dev/bncc-dados)** — the **Base Nacional
Comum Curricular as structured, verified data: `1,721` aprendizagens** (`1,580` across the
three stages of basic education plus `141` from the Computing complement, *Parecer CNE/CEB
2/2022*), in **JSON, CSV and SQLite**, each record carrying competencies, organisation
contexts and provenance, checked against the official document. **Code MIT, data CC BY 4.0**,
Zenodo **DOI `10.5281/zenodo.21625233`**, **npm and PyPI packages with the data embedded
that work with no internet**, and a **local MCP server** (`npx -y @bncc/mcp`). 🔵 Its
licence note states the legal basis — BNCC normative texts are official acts and not
copyrightable under **art. 8º, IV, Lei 9.610/98** — so the CC BY 4.0 covers the compilation
and curation, held by **Profy Tecnologias Educacionais Ltda**.

🟢 **And beside it, a measurement instrument:**
**[`bncc-dev/bncc-benchmark`](https://github.com/bncc-dev/bncc-benchmark)** — an open
**LLM-hallucination benchmark against the BNCC**, `15,300` responses from `17` models.
**Dual-licensed: MIT for `harness/` and `test/`, CC BY 4.0 for the item bank, results and
methodology**, with an explicit warning that the stored model responses were generated by
third-party models and carry their own reuse conditions. 🔵 **This is the only
jurisdiction-specific evaluation harness on these shelves**, and it is what makes
**`P-578-A`** sellable: a Brazilian deployment can be *measured* against the national
curriculum, not merely claimed to align with it.

🔵 **Carried, still the best-corroborated regional claim here:** **`92 %` of students and
`79 %` of faculty** engaging with AI (DEC + Tec de Monterrey, 2026) against an
institutional-governance gap independently measured by **UNESCO IESALC** (`200`
institutions, `19` countries, fielded Aug–Oct 2025). 🟢 **New this pass:** a **UNESCO IESALC
/ UNU-IAS working paper dated September 2026** maps AI adoption across Latin American and
Caribbean universities on teaching, research, community engagement, administration and
governance — a second mapping instrument, independent of the first.

#### 🔴 `p574` — Brazil's digital accessibility duty exists, its standard exists, and its REGULATION does not — plus a refutation of a date this pass nearly published

🔴 **The claim this block was nearly built on is false.** A 2026 Brazilian trade
article frames **`11 March`** as *"um marco na acessibilidade digital do
Brasil"*, and read quickly that looks like a compliance deadline in the current
year. 🟢 **It is a commemorative date**: `PL 2251/2025` in the Senate creates a
**Dia Nacional da Acessibilidade Digital**, and states have already enacted it
(Pernambuco, `Lei 19.201/2026`). 🔵 Recorded because the next pass will meet the
same article: **there is no `11 March 2026` Brazilian accessibility deadline.**

🟢 **What is actually true, and it is a better sales position than a deadline:**

| element | state |
| --- | --- |
| 🟢 The duty | **`LBI` (Lei 13.146/2015) art. 63** obliges digital accessibility on the sites of **companies headquartered in Brazil and government bodies alike** — in force since 2015 |
| 🟢 The technical standard | **`ABNT NBR 17225`**, published **`11 March 2025`** — a real national web-accessibility standard |
| 🔴 The regulation | **MISSING.** Art. 63 has never been regulated; making the ABNT standard a binding reference requires a **federal decree** that does not exist |
| 🔴 The enforcement | **Prosecutorial.** The **MPF** has recommended the federal government regulate art. 63 within **45 days** and is litigating to have the courts impose that duty; the public prosecutor's office is the single most active actor in Brazilian digital-accessibility cases |

🔵 **So the LATAM engagement is procurement-and-litigation-driven, not
calendar-driven** — the opposite of North America. The buyer is an institution
that wants to be defensible before a *promotoria* inquiry, and the deliverable is
the same evidence trail `P-576-A` builds, with no date to point at.

🟢 **A statutory channel North America and EMEA do not have, and it changes which
repository you may use.** **Libras** — Brazilian Sign Language — is statutory
(`Lei 10.436/2002`, `Decreto 5.296/2004`), so sign-language support is a
*procurement item* in Brazil rather than a nice-to-have. 🔴 **And this is exactly
where the layer's licensing bites:** the field's flagship,
[`sign/translate`](https://github.com/sign/translate) (`797★`), is
**CC BY-NC-SA** — noncommercial, so it cannot be in a paid deliverable. 🟢 **The
substitute is first-hand verified:**
[`sign-language-translator/sign-language-translator`](https://github.com/sign-language-translator/sign-language-translator)
— **Apache-2.0**, licence file `11,364 B`, `pyproject.toml` agrees, `380★`,
designed precisely for building a translator for a *specific* sign language.

🟡 **One regional evidence item from the mandated leg, grade `C`:** a **UNESCO
IESALC** working paper with **UNU-IAS**, dated **September 2026**, mapping AI use
across Latin American and Caribbean universities in five areas — teaching,
research, community engagement, administration and governance. 🔵 Worth a primary
read next pass; the publication itself was not fetched.

🔴 **Supply: `0` of the layer's `29` addresses are LATAM-origin.** Stated as a
gap, not left silent — and consistent with `p572`'s finding that this base's
origin instrument can only place **institutional** publishers.


#### 🟡 `p572` — the missing LATAM *classroom* rates, found where `p571` said to look, at grade `C†`

🟢 **The gap was real and the prescribed remedy worked. `p571` recorded: "query
CETIC.br and ministries, stop re-running legs" — the targeted query returned
quantified classroom adoption; the generic regional leg, run again this pass,
returned none.**

🔵 **And the reason this sat open for two passes is now clear: it was never a
missing *source*, it was a missing *population*.** This page already carried
Cetic.br at **53% of school administrators using AI, 22% reporting school
guidelines**. What was absent was the **classroom**: students and teachers.

| population | figure | instrument | grade |
| --- | --- | --- | --: |
| High-school students (internet users) using generative AI for schoolwork | **~70%** ("seven in ten") | **TIC Educação**, Cetic.br / NIC.br | 🟡 **C†** |
| Those who received school guidance on that use | **32%** | same | 🟡 **C†** |
| Teachers using generative AI in school activities | **58%** | relayed by *O Povo* **attributing** TIC Educação | 🔴 **D — do not quote** |
| School administrators (already on this page) | **53%** use / **22%** guidelines | Cetic.br | 🔵 B |

🔴 **Why `C†` and not `A`, stated plainly: `cetic.br` does not resolve from this
session.** It is outside the sandbox's permitted network domains
(`getaddrinfo ENOTFOUND`), so **the primary was not read** — these figures come
through search-result relay, with the `~70%` corroborated by the Cetic.br release
*title* itself ("Sete em cada dez alunos do Ensino Médio usam IA generativa em
pesquisas escolares, revela TIC Educação"). 🔴 **The `58%` teacher figure is
single-secondary and the search's own summary could not confirm it against
Cetic.br — it is recorded as `D` and must not be quoted to a client.**

🔴 **An unresolved conflict, registered rather than papered over (`572-D`):** this
page attributes its administrator figures to **"Cetic.br (2026)"**, while the
edition reachable this pass is **TIC Educação 2024**, with its book PDF stamped
`2025-12-17` and the sectoral study `2025-11-24`. 🔵 Either the page's `(2026)` is
a publication-year label for 2024 fieldwork, or there are two editions in play. **A
survey year and a publication year are different things and this KB is currently
asserting one as the other.** Resolving it needs the primary, which needs
`cetic.br` allowed in the session's network settings.

🟢 **What it is worth even at `C†`:** the student-to-guidance spread — **~70%
using, 32% guided** — is a **38-point** governance gap at school level that
mirrors, almost exactly, the **61-point** gap UNESCO measured in LATAM higher
education (87% use, 26% strategy). 🔵 **Two independent instruments, two different
education tiers, the same shape.** That is the single most quotable LATAM finding
in this KB, and it is now evidenced at both tiers rather than one.

**The best-evidenced adoption-governance gap in the world, from the most
authoritative source in the KB.** The **UNESCO IESALC** study (with UNU-IAS),
launched at **Digital Learning Week 2026** at UNESCO HQ in Paris, surveyed **200
higher-education institutions across 19 Latin American and Caribbean countries**:
**87% already use AI in at least one area of activity, and only 26% have a formal
AI strategy.** **55% of universities have no specific AI budget**, and public
universities have the lowest rate of dedicated AI funding at **4%**. A UNU working
paper (1 September 2026, Arianna Valentini) maps adoption across five dimensions:
teaching and learning, research, community engagement, administration, and
governance.

School-level data is national and primary. **Cetic.br (2026)**: **53% of Brazilian
school administrators use AI, while only 22% report that their school has
guidelines**. UNESCO reports **more than 50% of teachers in Chile and Brazil**
already use these tools while **fewer than 10% of institutions region-wide have
formal guidelines**. **Ceibal (Uruguay)** reports **75% of public-school teachers**
using them — the highest national figure located anywhere.

**Regulation: volume without binding force.** A 2026 Universidad de los Andes
study identified **716 AI-related regulatory instruments, rules and legislative
proposals across 23 countries and territories, more than 80% initiated since
2023**. Education-specific binding law is almost nonexistent: **Peru's Ley 31814
plus DS 115-2025-PCM** remains the only binding instrument naming education;
Chile's MINEDUC *PotencIA el aprendizaje* is guidance.

**Brazil's PL 2338/2023 is not law, and its status is now pinned.** It passed the
**Senate in December 2024** and sits in the **Câmara dos Deputados** special
committee **awaiting the rapporteur's opinion** — Deputy **Aguinaldo Ribeiro
(PP-PB)**. Roughly **37 proposals are attached** to it; the last recorded
movements (20 July – 2 September 2026) are attachments including **PL 3060/2026**,
with nothing new recorded as of early October. On **24 August 2026** the
rapporteur stated the vote would wait until **after the October 2026 elections**.
It still needs a Câmara plenary vote, and any textual change sends it back to the
Senate. *Evidentiary caveat: the official Câmara record could not be read from
this session — see Declared gaps.*

Institutional players are unusually well defined: **UNESCO IESALC** (study),
**UNESCO's Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean**
(launched **14 April 2026, Santiago, Chile**), **Cetic.br** and **Ceibal**
(national data), **CENIA** (Chile) and **Tecnológico de Monterrey** (UNESCO
partnerships), and the **Digital Education Council**, which publishes an *AI in
Higher Education LATAM Survey 2026* in English and Spanish.

**Opportunities.**
1. **AI strategy and governance for universities — the single clearest
   opportunity in this KB.** 87% use it; 26% have a strategy. The gap is 61
   points, measured by UNESCO, in a region where Globant already has the deepest
   presence. It is advisory work, not platform work, and the 55%-no-budget figure
   says to price it accordingly.
2. **Brazilian public-sector school management.** `portabilis/i-educar` (GPL-2.0)
   runs municipal *Secretarias de Educação* nationwide. Copyleft, so attach via
   LTI/MCP rather than fork — and Cetic.br's 53%/22% split is the business case
   for the governance layer on top.
3. **A compliance window that is still open.** With PL 2338 stalled past the
   October elections and no education-specific instrument outside Peru, clients
   can build governance before being compelled to. That is a *preparedness* sale,
   not a *compliance* sale, and it should be positioned honestly as such.
4. **Spanish- and Portuguese-language assets.** `Chamilo` has a strong LATAM
   install base; `ltijs` has a Brazilian maintainer; **`dio-agent`** (2,750★,
   Portuguese) proves the regional demand for study-mentor agents — **but it is
   unlicensed** (see [`../agents/top.md`](../agents/top.md)), so it is evidence,
   not a component.

**Added on the fifth pass — two new sources, and the budget finding is the one
to sell on.**

- **An October 2026 regional AI index across 20 LATAM economies gives the
  governance gap a sharper edge than the institutional data did.** **85%** of
  countries have a national AI strategy in place or under development and **75%**
  have formally designated a lead institution — but **only Brazil and Colombia
  explicitly allocate budget** within their national instruments. Top tier:
  **Brazil, Chile and Uruguay**. More than half of countries have incorporated AI
  into school curricula. **The pattern mirrors the institutional finding one level
  up:** strategy without funding at country level, exactly as UNESCO IESALC found
  87% use against 26% strategy and 55% with no AI budget at institution level.
  **Two independent levels of the same system show the same defect** — the
  artefact exists, the money does not — which is a far stronger basis for a
  funding-and-roadmap engagement than either finding alone.
- **The Digital Education Council's LATAM 2026 survey corroborates adoption at
  the individual level: 92% of students and 79% of faculty actively engaging with
  AI**, from **over 30,000 responses across 29 higher-education institutions**,
  produced with **Tecnológico de Monterrey's Institute for the Future of
  Education**. Note what this adds rather than repeats: IESALC measured
  *institutions*; this measures *people inside them*, and the two agree. Tec de
  Monterrey's involvement also makes it a citeable regional partner rather than
  an external observer.
- **UNESCO IESALC's causal reading should be quoted directly in pitches:**
  adoption is being driven by **faculty, researchers and students**, not by
  institutional policy or strategic guidance. The buyer is therefore being asked
  to catch up with their own staff — which is a different conversation from
  being asked to innovate, and an easier one to fund.
- ⚠️ **A widening-inequality signal, October 2026:** regional reporting describes
  Latin America facing AI inequality as **compute and adoption gaps widen**. This
  cuts against the offline/low-resource optimism elsewhere in this KB and argues
  for the **offline-first and local-inference** assets (`Kolibri`, `tesseract`,
  `PAIDEIA`, `WordLink`'s in-browser model) rather than hosted-model designs in
  the region's less-resourced systems.


**Sixth pass (`p568`) — 🔴 zero new addresses, and the demand-side case is
stronger than the supply-side one.**

- 🔴 **Supply: nothing new this pass.** Four regional WebSearch legs returned no
  address new to this base. The only LATAM-placed education agent on this base,
  [`digitalinnovationone/dio-agent`](https://github.com/digitalinnovationone/dio-agent)
  (2,750★), is still **ungranted** — verified by three methods — and the
  `measured-unusable` register still holds `ceanjonnube-negra/docente-ia`
  (Mexico, SEP-scoped, **no grant**). 🔴 **The region's local-supply story and its
  build-on-it story remain about different repositories**, and that must not be
  blurred in a market slide.
- 🟢 **Demand: the governance gap is the engagement, and it is now measured by a
  credible source.** UNESCO launched its **Observatory on AI in Education for
  Latin America and the Caribbean** in **Santiago on 14 April 2026**, reporting
  that in countries such as Chile and Brazil **more than 50% of teachers already
  use these tools while fewer than 10% of institutions have formal guidelines**.
  🟢 That asymmetry — adoption without policy — is precisely what a studio is paid
  to close, and it is an advisory-plus-platform engagement rather than a licence
  sale.
- 🟢 **The assets that answer it are already on this base, and two are new this
  pass.** An auditable assessment-and-telemetry trail a *secretaria* can show a
  regulator: `itembank` (**MIT**, local-first, no model in the scoring path) for
  defensible marking, plus `ralph` (**MIT**) for an xAPI record of what was
  actually delivered, attached to `i-educar` (GPL-2.0) — the reference Brazilian
  municipal platform — over a standard rather than a fork.
- 🔵 **Funding and market shape:** Google.org committed **$4.6M** with the
  Raspberry Pi Foundation to scale *Experience AI* across nine countries
  (Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Dominican Republic, El Salvador, Mexico,
  Peru, Uruguay), targeting **1.25M students by 2028**; HolonIQ's **2026 LATAM
  EdTech 100** has **Brazil at roughly half the cohort**. 🔵 Regulation: Brazil's
  **PL 2338/2023** remains the anchor framework with an algorithmic-impact-assessment
  duty for high-risk deployers and the **ANPD** coordinating the national AI
  governance system; Mexico's bill carries a digital-rights chapter and no
  explicit prohibition tier. 🔴 Bill status moves — confirm against the Senado and
  the Mexican Congress before a client commitment.
- 🔵 **Counterweight already on this page:** compute and adoption gaps are
  *widening* in the region, which argues for the offline-first assets
  (`Kolibri`, `tesseract`, `PAIDEIA`, `WordLink`, and now
  `Changochen/slide-to-video`'s local voice cloning) over hosted-model designs in
  less-resourced systems.


#### 🆕 `p569` (`2026-10-31`) — two rows found, one unshelvable, and the region's structural disadvantage now has a measurement

🟢 **[`JaxisReal/Moodle-Reporte-Limpio`](https://github.com/JaxisReal/Moodle-Reporte-Limpio)**
— **MIT** (`main/LICENSE`, 1,086 B, *"Copyright (c) 2026 Jerry Anderson Carril
Chávez"*), 9 commits, 2 authors. A Moodle plugin for generating, processing and
visualising **course-completion reports**, Spanish-language. 🔵 The holder is an
individual, so **by grant it is `Global`; by artefact it is LATAM.** Useful as a
concrete starting point for a *secretaría* that already runs Moodle and wants
completion reporting before it wants AI.

🔴 **[`smatilio/ic-tri-simulacao`](https://github.com/smatilio/ic-tri-simulacao)
cannot be shelved, and this is the most actionable gap on the page.** It is a
Brazilian **TRI** (item response theory) simulation study — *"2PL longitudinal
with a human-machine effect, estimated with `mirt` and Stan"*, R, 16 commits,
pushed `2026-10-30`. 🔴 **It carries NO licence at 5 filenames × 2 branches**, so
it is unusable regardless of quality. 🟢 **The remedy is a message, not an
engagement:** this is a live academic repository whose author has very likely not
considered the question, and a polite issue asking for a licence is the
single highest-yield action available on LATAM supply this pass. 🔵 Registered as
part of `569-F`.

🟢 **And the structural point, now measured rather than asserted.** This base has
recorded thin LATAM supply for many passes. 🔴 **Part of that thinness is an
artefact of how it searched.** A star-sorted sweep systematically under-reports
projects that are non-English, single-author and unlaunched — which describes
most LATAM and APAC education supply. 🟢 **`topic:ai-tutor` holds `793` of its
`816` results below 50 stars, and three of this pass's four high-commit finds
are non-English-first.** 🔵 **The correction is `P-572-C`, and LATAM is where it
pays most.**

🔵 **Regional read (secondary, but the primary source here is strong).** The
**UNESCO IESALC / UNU-IAS** working paper (**1 September 2026**) maps AI use
across LAC universities on five dimensions — teaching, research, community
engagement, administration, governance — and remains the best-evidenced regional
item in this KB (the 87% / 26% / 55% figures above). **ECLAC/CEPAL's ILIA index**
puts **Chile, Brazil and Mexico** ahead regionally with persistent gaps in data,
training and regulation; Baker McKenzie confirms countries sit at **very
different regulatory stages**, so a multi-country LATAM engagement cannot assume
one compliance posture. Commercial actors named: **Platzi**, and a Huawei
education alliance launched 2025. The **World Bank's WDR 2026** is on AI.

🔴 **Declared gap: the LATAM leg returned no K-12 AI policy for any individual
country and no repository-level asset.** Both country-level K-12 policy (Brazil,
Mexico) and LATAM repository supply remain unmeasured by search; the two rows
above came from the `stars:<50` band, not from the regional leg.

#### 🟢 `p573` (2026-10-31) — the policy deficit is now measured, and it is the product

🔴 **Student adoption is near-total and institutional policy is near-absent.** A
Digital Education Council survey with **over 30,000 responses** reports **92 % of
students using AI regularly and 67 % at least weekly**, alongside an explicit
finding of **a lack of formal AI-use policies in Latin American universities**.

🔴 **Country detail:**
- **Mexico** — *"no existe una ley o regulación nacional que oriente su
  implementación ni lineamientos obligatorios."* One 2026 study claims **over
  80 % of Mexican higher-education institutions lack a clear normative
  framework**; ⚠️ **single weak source, figure not independently confirmed** —
  carry the direction, not the number.
- **Brazil** — the **general AI bill sits in the Chamber of Deputies**, expected
  to come into force in 2026. Brazil and Colombia have advanced **national AI
  strategies but no education-sector regulation.**
- **Regional** — a 2026 academic review of **52 policy documents** concludes the
  region shows **significant regulatory fragmentation**, with many universities
  **adopting international frameworks without the capacity to implement them.**

🟢 **The clearest institutional signal this pass: on 27 October 2026 in Madrid,
rectors of around twenty Ibero-American universities publicly called for shared
regulation of AI**, together with language and mobility measures. 🔵 Four days
before this pass — the freshest demand signal in this KB, and it comes from the
buyers themselves.

🟢 **Commercial consequence, and it is different in kind from the other three
regions: here the gap is not tooling and not law — it is implementation
capacity.** An institution that has adopted a UNESCO or EU-shaped framework it
cannot operationalise does not need a model; it needs the policy turned into
procedures, controls and staff training. 🟢 **That is the billable unit, and
`P-568-E` already describes it** — this pass supplies the evidence that the
demand is institutional and self-declared rather than inferred from student
behaviour.


#### 🆕 `p575` — 2026-10-31 — 🟢 the LATAM number this pass found is **`92 % / 79 %` adoption against near-absent institutional governance**, and that gap *is* the engagement

🟢 **The Digital Education Council's "AI in Higher Education LATAM Survey 2026"**, run
with **Tecnológico de Monterrey**, reports **`92 %` of students and `79 %` of faculty
actively engaging with AI.** Teacher optimism rose from **`57 %` to `72 %`** in 2026,
though most teachers report only moderate use. 🔴 **And a participant's own words name
the gap: "a lack of formal policies for the use of AI in universities in Latin
America."**

🟢 **A second, independent source measures the same gap at institutional scale.**
**UNESCO IESALC** surveyed **200 institutions across 19 countries**, fielded **August
to October 2025**, and found the gap between adoption and institutional governance
**"particularly pronounced"** in the region. 🔵 **Two sources, different methods, same
finding — this is the best-corroborated regional claim on this page.**

🔵 **Read the two together and the LATAM proposition is unusually clean.** Adoption is
*already* near-universal in higher education; what does not exist is the governance
layer — AI-use policy, assessment-integrity rules, data boundaries, staff guidance,
accessibility duty. 🟢 **So the LATAM engagement is not "adopt AI", it is "govern the
AI your institution already runs"**, and it can be sold with the client's own
adoption numbers. 🔴 **This is the opposite of the North America framing above**
(a sanctioned-tooling gap at the *student tooling* level) and should not be pitched
the same way.

🟢 **Named sources and instruments for the region, as a market map:**
- **UNESCO IESALC** — the 200-institution / 19-country regional survey.
- **UNU-IAS + UNESCO IESALC** — a working paper mapping AI across teaching, research,
  administration and governance in LAC universities.
- **ILIA (Latin America AI Index), 3rd edition, 19 countries** — tracks readiness,
  adoption and governance; data co-hosted by the **IDB**, published with **CEPAL**.
  🟢 The one enumerated, multi-country, non-vendor instrument this base has for LATAM.
- **Digital Education Council** + **Tecnológico de Monterrey** / **Institute for the
  Future of Education** — the adoption survey above.
- **Brookings** (May 2025) argues the region is a natural sandbox for inclusive AI
  governance and flags **education** as an area where flawed design could reinforce
  inequality.

🔴 **The standing LATAM gap is unchanged and was searched again: no 2026 national
regulatory text or education-ministry rule was reached for Brazil, Mexico or Chile.**
🔵 The leg returns regional surveys and multilateral commentary, never a ministry
instrument — **the same failure the APAC leg had until this pass broke it with
per-jurisdiction queries.** 🟢 **Ask: run that same per-jurisdiction method here**
(Brazil MEC + `PL 2338/2023`, Mexico SEP, Chile's national AI policy), registered as
`575-E`. The APAC result this pass is the evidence that the method works.

🔴 **And the figure `574` flagged stays flagged:** the LATAM market estimate on offer
in earlier passes was off by **three orders of magnitude**. Nothing this pass served
corrects it, so **no LATAM market *size* figure is published on this page** — only the
adoption and governance measurements above, which are first-party survey outputs
rather than vendor extrapolations.
### Global

#### 🆕 `p580` — `2026-11-01` — the unplaceable rows, and why region placement got harder this pass

🟢 **Re-measured first-hand:** `oppia/oppia` (**Apache-2.0**, `6,951★`, `sdg-4`) — 🟢 the
largest permissive education platform in this base; `DSpace/DSpace` (**BSD-3-Clause**,
`1,105★`); `adlnet/xAPI-Spec` (**Apache-2.0**, `956★`, last pushed `2025-07-03` — 🔵 stale
by date and legitimately so, because a ratified specification should sit still);
`vardanagarwal/Proctoring-AI` (**MIT**, `633★`).

🔴 **A channel limit that bears directly on this page's regional discipline.** The GitHub
`users/{login}` endpoint returns **`403`** from this harness — the same repository-allowlist
boundary that blocks `repos/{owner}/{repo}` — so owner `location` and `company`, the
natural way to place a repository by region, are **unreachable**. Every placement this
pass made was derived from **description language, topics and institutional names in
prose**, and the two new rows (`lama-loca` → EMEA from Russian; `BCTK` → APAC from
Chinese) are placed on that basis and labelled as such.

🔵 **What this means for the `region` field going forward:** it is an inference from text,
not a lookup, for any repository without an institutional name. Rows that cannot be placed
belong here under `Global` **with the reason stated**, rather than being guessed into a
region — which is the failure mode that makes a region filter stop working.


**p579 — 2026-11-01.**

🔴 **Assessment generation has no anchor anywhere.** `topic:exam-generator`: `73`
repositories, head `93★`. Against `topic:flashcards` at `3,274` and `topic:edtech` at
`4,980`, the layer every engagement must deliver is the one open source has not built. Both
permissive heads (`exameow` Apache-2.0 `93★`, `BCTK` MIT `53★`) are offline-first **desktop
applications**, not embeddable libraries. **Opportunity, and it is a build:** assessment
generation as a service, on `Numbas` (Apache-2.0, `216★`) for the item model and
`scorm-again` (MIT, `362★`) for interchange. 🔵 This is the second pass running to find the
gap at the artefact closest to an institution's obligations — `p578` found `topic:iep`
topping out at `7★`. The pattern is becoming a thesis: **open source builds what learners
use and skips what institutions are accountable for.**

**p577 — 2026-10-31.** 2026 global market estimates span **≈US$3.7B to ≈US$11.4B**
across research firms — a ~3× spread of incompatible methodologies, not a
consensus. K-12 reported at ~34% share. The **OECD Digital Education Outlook 2026**
warns AI improves learning only where pedagogy stays in control, which is the
citation for an evidence-first engagement narrative.
**The cross-region architectural constant:** the agent layer is permissive (15 of
16 verified agents are MIT/Apache/BSD) while every LMS of record is copyleft
(GPL/AGPL/LGPL). Attach, never fork — `ltijs` (Apache-2.0) is the keystone in
every region. See `repos/foundations.md`.
**The global capability gap:** one verified pedagogy benchmark against sixteen
agents. The industry builds tutors faster than it can show they teach — and a
studio that can measure learning gain is differentiated everywhere.


- 🔴 **Read `Global` on this page as "unplaced", and know that `p570` found a
  structural reason it is over-counted.** Region here is recovered from the
  copyright line in a licence file. 🔴 **Apache-2.0 has no copyright line** — its
  template never asks for one — so **four of four** Apache rows added this pass
  carry no holder and are unplaceable *by construction*, whichever country wrote
  them. 🔵 **The consequence for anyone reading the regional counts on this page
  as a market measurement: `Global` is inflated by whichever licence family a
  layer happens to prefer, and the rubric/evaluation layer prefers Apache-2.0.**
  Five of its seven rows are `Global` for that reason alone. Registered as
  `570-C`.
- 🔴 **And the five-value vocabulary has no Africa value, so EMEA is a composite
  bucket.** `hanskepper/SMART-GRADE` is a **Cameroonian** school gradebook and is
  recorded `EMEA†` because there is nowhere else to put it. 🔵 **Any EMEA figure
  quoted off this page should state whether it includes African rows**, or a
  German research institute and an African school-IT project are being counted as
  one market. The vocabulary is closed on purpose and should stay closed — the
  fix is the `†` marker and the country in prose, not a sixth value. Registered
  as `570-E`.
- **The attachment layer is the product**, everywhere, for licence reasons that
  do not vary by region: LTI 1.3 (`ltijs`, Apache-2.0), MCP (MIT servers), xAPI →
  LRS (`xAPI-Spec` Apache-2.0, `ralph` MIT).
- **Quality gating is an unserved global need.** `learning-commons-org/evaluators`
  (rubrics, licence-unresolved) and `pedagogy-benchmark` (MIT) are the only
  verified evaluation assets in existence for education AI, and **neither is a
  runtime monitor**. Every region's governance gap is, concretely, this missing
  capability.
- **Agent skills travel across borders and licences do not.** The skill cohort is
  overwhelmingly APAC-origin and mostly MIT, but the largest member is **CC BY-SA
  4.0** (share-alike). Licence-screen every skill bundle before it enters a
  client deliverable.

#### 🟢 `p573` (2026-10-31) — adoption figures, and the first large RCT this KB can cite

🟢 **Adoption, Digital Education Council (published July 2026): student AI use at
88 %, faculty at 77 %.** 🔵 Consistent with this page's standing finding, and the
faculty figure is the one that has moved — the student/faculty gap is closing.

🟢 **The efficacy evidence this KB has been missing now exists.** A **University
of Maryland working paper (October 2026)** reports a **randomised trial of 2,379
undergraduates** using a GPT-4o tutor inside course platforms. 🔴 **The result is
cautionary: tutor *design* determines whether learning improves or degrades** —
coverage frames it as AI tutors having both beaten good teaching and cut grades,
depending on design.

🔵 **Why this matters more than a market figure.** Every efficacy claim in this KB
has until now rested on vendor studies or single-institution pilots. 🟢 **This is
a large randomised trial with a null-or-negative arm, which is precisely the
evidence an institutional buyer's academic board asks for.** 🟢 **It converts
`P-570-C` (efficacy trial for a tutor already in production) from a
nice-to-have into a defensible, citable necessity** — and it is the strongest
available argument against shipping a tutor without an evaluation harness.
⚠️ **Working paper, not yet peer-reviewed; read before quoting.**


## Declared gaps — what was searched and not found

### Closed this pass

- ~~**APAC rests on vendor market research with no primary survey.**~~ **Closed.**
  TEQSA's **100%-response** institutional census, the peer-reviewed **AJET**
  39-university document analysis (7/39), **MEXT's** pilot counts (149
  municipalities, 478 schools, <2%) and administrative-use figure (17.2%, +14.5
  pp), and Singapore's **MOE Rapid Research Fund** commission are all primary or
  near-primary. *What made the difference:* searching the **regulator and
  ministry** (TEQSA, MEXT, MOE) rather than the region — the same lesson that
  closed the EMEA gap by searching "European Commission" instead of "EMEA".
- ~~**South Africa's National AI Policy status is unresolved.**~~ **Closed.**
  Gazetted 10 April 2026 (Notice 3880), withdrawal announced 26 April 2026,
  formal Gazette withdrawal 12 June 2026, cause: ≥10% fabricated academic
  references. The 2024 Framework stands without statutory force.
- ~~**Brazil's PL 2338 status is contradicted across three sources.**~~ **Closed
  as far as this session can reach**, and the three sources no longer contradict:
  passed Senate Dec 2024, in Câmara special committee awaiting Deputy Aguinaldo
  Ribeiro's opinion, ~37 proposals attached, last movement 2 September 2026, vote
  deferred past the October 2026 elections, **not law**. See the caveat below.

### Still open

- **The official Câmara dos Deputados record could not be read from this
  session.** `www.camara.leg.br` does not resolve here — it is outside the
  session's allowed network domains. The PL 2338 account above rests on
  consultancy and law-firm trackers that **agree on every material point**, which
  is why it is recorded as closed-with-caveat rather than closed. A session with
  that domain allowed should confirm the *ficha de tramitação* directly.
- **Market-size figures remain entirely vendor outputs.** Three passes, no
  independent estimate. The USD 10.6–12.5 bn range stands as a range, and the
  2030/CAGR figures inherit the same provenance.
- **EU AI Act primary text cannot be read from this session, and that is now
  established rather than deferred.** The third pass named reading the Official
  Journal "the highest-value single action available to the next pass". It was
  attempted this pass and it is **not achievable from here**:
  `eur-lex.europa.eu` does **not resolve**, and neither does the
  `artificialintelligenceact.eu` mirror — the same failure class as
  `www.camara.leg.br`. This is a **network-policy constraint, not a research
  gap**, and it should stop being queued as an action item: it is a request to
  allow the domain in the session's network settings. Everything this page says
  about **Regulation (EU) 2026/1744**, the 2 December 2027 Annex III date, the
  2 August 2028 embedded-product date, the undeferred Article 50 duties and the
  Article 4 literacy obligation remains **multi-source secondary**, consistent
  across independent legal commentaries, and unverified against the OJ.
- **South Africa has no replacement draft** and no announced date beyond
  FY2026/27.
- **Singapore's MOE teacher/student AI survey is commissioned but unpublished.**
  Dated watch item: it will be the region's first government teacher-use survey.
- **African supply side is mapped but tiny.** `AI-for-Education` (MIT, LMIC-focused)
  closes the "nothing exists" gap, but every repo is 0–13★ and the
  organisation's own **location is not stated**. African-*domiciled* publishers
  remain unmapped.
- **A continental "USD 3 bn" African AI/STEM rollout figure is advocacy, not
  official** — still excluded from the numbers above.
- **LATAM returned nothing new this pass, and that is recorded rather than left
  silent.** The prescribed regional search
  (`AI education LATAM Latin America 2026 adoption regulation players`) returned
  the UNESCO IESALC study, the Observatory launch, Ceibal, Cetic.br, the
  Universidad de los Andes instrument count and Tecnológico de Monterrey —
  **every one of which was already on this page from the previous pass**. No new
  institution, instrument or figure. For the region where Globant has the deepest
  presence, the evidence base is now **stable rather than growing**, which is a
  different thing from under-researched: the 87/26 gap has been the headline for
  three passes and nothing has moved it.
- **The Council of Europe education instrument is single-source.** Named,
  dated 2026, and carried as a watch item, not an obligation.
- **The Ipsos Education Monitor 2026 ban-support figures are single-source** and
  the earlier comparison year is not stated in what was read, so the shifts are
  directionally reliable and the baselines are not pinned.
- **No independent audit of the UNESCO IESALC figures.** They are the most
  authoritative numbers on this page and they are single-source. The 26%
  strategy figure in particular was reported as "about a quarter" by one relay
  and **26%** by another; 26% is used here.
